Thermal boundary conditions in fractional superdiffusion of energy Thanks: T.K acknowledges the support of the NCN grant 2024/53/B/ST1/00286
Tomasz Komorowski, Stefano Olla

Abstract
. We study heat conduction in a one-dimensional finite, unpinned chain of atoms perturbed by stochastic momentum exchange and coupled to Langevin heat baths at possibly distinct temperatures placed at the endpoints of the chain. While infinite systems without boundaries are known to exhibit superdiffusive energy transport described by a fractional heat equation with the generator − | Δ | 3 / 4 -|\Delta|^{3/4} , the corresponding boundary conditions induced by heat baths remain less understood. We establish the hydrodynamic limit for a finite chain with n + 1 n+1 atoms connected to thermostats at the endpoints, deriving the macroscopic evolution of the averaged energy profile. The limiting equation is governed by a non-local Lévy-type operator, with boundary terms determined by explicit interaction kernels that encode absorption, reflection, and transmission of long-wavelength phonons at the baths. Our results provide the first rigorous identification of boundary conditions for fractional superdiffusion arising directly from microscopic dynamics with local interactions, highlighting their distinction from both diffusive and pinned-chain settings.



1.
Introduction

Heat conduction in dielectric materials involves the transfer of energy through
the vibrations
of atoms, which generate waves
that propagate throughout the material.
Heat superdiffusion is generically expected in acoustic
(unpinned) one-dimensional chains of atoms,
where the dispersion relation allows long waves to travel with non-vanishing velocity.
Thermal conductivity is defined by the Green-Kubo formula that involves space-time
correlations of the dynamics.
Numerical evidence suggests that the thermal conductivity diverges with the system size
in chains of non-linear oscillators
[
23
,
24
]
.
Anharmonic deterministic Hamiltonian dynamics are mathematically difficult and at the moment
there are no rigorous results in this direction. Even the convergence or the divergence of the
Green-Kubo formula is not proven.
Rigorous mathematical results were obtained for acoustic
harmonic
chains
with a random exchange
of velocities between nearest-neighboring atoms. Such a stochastic
mechanism emulates elastic
collisions between the atoms.
It conserves the total energy
and momentum while
breaking the complete integrability of the harmonic chain,
thus induces scattering of the waves.
The corresponding scattering rates are inversely proportional
to the wave length, which, in turn, induces a macroscopic
fractional Lévy superdiffusion of energy,
carried predominantely by the long waves.
This behaviour contrasts with that of optical (pinned)
chains, where long waves propagate
slowly and energy diffusivity is finite.
The divergence of thermal conductivity in stochastically perturbed
acoustic chains was proven in
[
1
,
2
]
.
A kinetic equation was derived in a low noise limit in

[
4
]
, and a superdiffusive
scaling limit from the kinetic equation was obtained in

[
11
,
3
]
, yielding a heat equation governed by the fractional Laplacian
|\Delta|^{3/4}
.
A direct space-time scaling limit from the microscopic dynamics
(i.e. the hydrodynamic limit), without relying on the kinetic equation, was first proven in
[
12
]
.
The aforementioned results concern infinite systems
without boundary conditions; a review can be found in
[
5
]
.

A natural question arises regarding
the boundary conditions that emerge when
heat baths are in contact with a chain whose dynamics leads
to a superdiffusive evolution of the energy.
In the diffusive case – e.g., when the chain is pinned or the noise does
not conserve momentum, see
[
16
,
17
]
– a heat bath generates
a fixed (Dirichlet) boundary condition determined by its
temperature.
The situation is more complicated in the case of a fractional diffusion. In fact, due
to the non-locality of the fractional Laplacian operator,
various boundary conditions can
be defined and it is not apriori
evident which one emerges from
the underlying microscopic dynamics.

According to heuristic physics literature, see
[
25
,
26
,
7
,
22
]
, the type of the macroscopic boundary condition
for the energy superdiffusion depends on whether the chain is microscopically pinned or unpinned
at the boundary.
In particular in
[
26
,
22
]
the authors characterize (heuristically)
the macroscopic boundary conditions when the chain is
pinned at its endpoints (i.e. the first and the last particle cannot move).
In the unpinned case (at the endpoints) boundary conditions involving boundary layers
are expected to appear,
but their exact formulation is not clear, see
[
26
,
7
]
. In the present paper we determine and mathematically justify the boundary conditions and the boundary layer
emerging from the unpinned dynamics.
Our method can be also applied to the pinned boundary case (in fact
the argument is somewhat simpler then),
providing a mathematical proof of the heuristic results of
[
26
,
22
]
.

Most of the numerical simulations of the anharmonic dynamics are performed on
open chains connected to heat baths at different temperatures
[
23
,
24
]
.
We expect that non-linear dynamics will macroscopically lead to a
superdiffusion, with boundary conditions similar
to those found here. This highlights the importance of obtaining a
precise formulation of
such conditions.
Furthermore, according to our knowledge, the particular boundary conditions for a fractional Laplacian, described in the present paper,
appear to be new in the existing mathematical literature.

Model.

A standard setup consists in a finite chain of
n+1
atoms with
two Langevin heat baths, at temperatures
T_{L}

and
T_{R}
, attached to the left and right endpoints, respectively.

We consider atoms labeled by
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}:=\{0,\ldots,n\}
, with positions

\mathbf{q}(t)=\big(q_{x}(t)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
and momenta

\mathbf{p}(t)=\big(p_{x}(t)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
where
q_{x}(t),p_{x}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
.
The dynamics is given by:

(1.1)

\begin{split}&\dot{q}_{x}(t)=p_{x}(t),\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n},\quad\text{and in the bulk for }x=1,\ldots,n-1,\\
&\mathrm{d}p_{x}(t)=\Delta_{\rm N}q_{x}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\big[\nabla^{\star}p_{x+1}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{x,x+1}(\gamma t)-\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{x-1,x}(\gamma t)\big].\end{split}

At the boundaries
x=0,n
the energy is exchanged with two
Langevin heat baths at temperatures
T_{L}>0
and
T_{R}>0
,
respectively:

(1.2)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}p_{0}(t)=\Delta_{\rm N}q_{0}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\nabla^{\star}p_{1}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{0,1}(\gamma t)-\tilde{\gamma}p_{0}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2T_{L}\tilde{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}w_{L}\\
&\\
&\mathrm{d}p_{n}(t)=\Delta_{\rm N}q_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\nabla^{*}p_{n}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{n-1,n}(\gamma t)-\tilde{\gamma}p_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2T_{R}\tilde{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}w_{R}(t)\end{split}

Here
w_{L}(t)
and
w_{R}(t)
are independent standard Brownian motions, and

\{N_{x,x+1}(t),x=0,\dots,n-1\}
are independent Poisson processes of intensity
1
,
independent of the Brownian motions. We denote
\nabla^{\star}f_{x}=f_{x}-f_{x-1}
.
The Neumann discrete Laplacian,
\Delta_{\rm N}
is defined as

\Delta_{\rm N}f_{x}=f_{x+1}+f_{x-1}-2f_{x}=\nabla\nabla^{\star}f_{x}
,
with boundary conditions

f_{n+1}:=f_{n}
and
f_{-1}=f_{0}
.
The parameters
\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}>0
determine
the respective rates of the momentum exchange and the strength
of the heat bath.

This dynamics is
unpinned
and consequently
invariant under
translations of the positions (
q_{x}\rightarrow q_{x}+a
,
a\in\mathbb{R}
).
It is therefore convenient to work with the
configuration space

(1.3)

(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p})=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n},p_{0},\dots,p_{n})\in\Omega_{n}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})

correspond to the inter-particle stretches
r_{x}:=q_{x}-q_{x-1}
,

x=1,\ldots,n
.
The momentum exchange mechanism guarantees that
the energy and momenta and stretches are locally conserved.

We assume that the initial data
(\mathbf{r}(0),\mathbf{p}(0))
is randomly
distributed according to some probability measure
\mu_{n}
on
\Omega_{n}
,
with zero means for both stretches and momenta.
We further assume certain regularity conditions on
\mu_{n}
, notably
that the relative entropy of
\mu_{n}
,
with respect to the Gibbs equilibrium measure at some temperature, is
bounded by a constant times the size
n
of the system.

Scaling limit

We study the averaged energy profile in the superdiffusive scaling

(1.4)

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left(r^{2}_{[nu]}(n^{3/2}t)+p^{2}_{[nu]}(n^{3/2}t)\right),\qquad u\in[0,1],t\geq 0.

Here
\mathbb{E}_{n}
denotes the expectation with respect to the
randomness coming from the initial data, Langevin thermostats and
momenta exchanges.
As shown in Theorem
2.8
, the averaged
energy profiles, viewed as measure-valued functions on
[0,1]
,
converge weakly, as
n\to\infty
, to the solution
T(t,u)
of the equation:

(1.5)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}T(t,u)&=\int_{0}^{1}r(u,u^{\prime})\left[T(t,u^{\prime})-T(t,u)\right]du^{\prime}+\sum_{v=0,1}b(u;v)\left[T_{v}-T(t,u)\right],\end{split}

with
T_{0}=T_{L}
and
T_{1}:=T_{R}
.

In (
3.13
) the coefficients
b(u;v)
,
v=0,1
represent the rates of absorption and creation at
u\in(0,1)

due to the heat baths. One can show, see Proposition
3.3
, that

b(u;v)\to+\infty
, for
u\to v
, where

v=0,1
, see Proposition
3.3
,
ensuring
the boundary conditions
T(t,v)=T_{v}
are satisfied.

The kernel
r(u,u^{\prime})
is symmetric (see (
3.15
)) and
determined by the jump rates of the Lévy-type process whose
generator is a nonlocal operator with a resolvent-dependent Robin boundary interaction
on
[0,1]
,
see Proposition
3.1
.

The above can be expressed equivalently as:

(1.6)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}T(t,u)=-c_{\rm bulk}|\Delta_{\rm N}|^{3/4}T(t,u)\\
&+c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,v)\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\frac{F(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\\
&-c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}V_{\varrho}(u,1-v)\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\,\frac{G(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})d\rho}{\rho^{3/4}},\end{split}

where
c_{\rm bulk},c_{\rm bd}>0
and functions

F(\cdot,\cdot)
,
G(\cdot,\cdot)
are given in (
2.29
)
and (
2.17
), res[ectively. The operator

-|\Delta_{\rm N}|^{3/4}
is the
spectral fractional power of the Neumann Laplacian on
[0,1]

and

V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},u)=\varrho G_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},u)
, where

G_{\varrho}=(\rho-\Delta_{\rm N})^{-1}
is its Green’s function.

Concerning the boundary condition, we require that for
v=0,1

(1.7)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\Big(\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)\big(T_{v}-T(s,u^{\prime})\big)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\Big)^{2}\Big\}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}<+\infty

for any
t>0
. The precise notion of a solution of (
1.6
)
and (
1.7
) is given in
Definition
2.2
.
The result informally described above is rigorously formulated in Theorem

2.8
below.

Context

As mentioned in the foregoing,
similar dynamics but with pinned boundaries (i.e. with the microscopic
Dirichlet Laplacian in
(
1.2
), where
f_{-1}=0
,
f_{n+1}=0
)
has been studied heuristically in
[
25
,
22
]
.
Because of the pinned microscopic boundary, different macroscopic
boundary conditions are expected. Namely, the second term on the right
hand side of (
1.6
) does not appear.
This could be understood as follows: the boundary pinning changes
locally the dispersion relation of the chain,
slowing down the long waves when they approach the boundary.
In that respect the limit obtained in the present paper differs from
the regional fractional Laplacian describing the superdiffusion
of the
density
of particles as formulated in
[
6
]
.
Notice that in
[
6
]
particles perform symmetric random walks
with long jumps and subject to the exclusion rule. This makes the dynamics
non-local already at the microscopic level.
Our dynamics is completely local on the microscopic level, non-locality emerges for the

energy
evolution at the macroscopic space-time scale.

Some intuition about the result can be construed from the
kinetic limit approach
.
It is a weak noise (or weak anharmonicity in the non-linear case) limit,
where the averaged Wigner distribution of the waves of a given frequency (mode or wave number
k
)
converges
to a density distribution of
phonons
in space-mode domain,
that evolves according to an inhomogeneous, linear
kinetic equation
[
4
]
. In the kinetic limit, for an unpinned chain, phonons of mode

k
move with positive velocity, almost independent of
k
,
and change the mode (
scatter
) with rate proportional to

k^{2}
. Consequently, most of the energy is carried
by low mode
k
phonons (long waves),
that, in the proper space-time scaling limit, perform a
3/2
-Lévy superdiffusion

[
11
,
3
]
.

The kinetic limit of the infinite dynamics with Langevin, or Poisson type heat baths
attached at a point was studied in
[
21
,
18
,
19
]
.
A phonon
can get absorbed, reflected, or transmitted when its
trajectory intersects the heat bath,
and can also be created at explicitly computable rate
depending on the wave number.
Then, starting from the kinetic equation, the
superdiffusive hydrodynamic limit has been obtained in

[
20
]
. It is
described by a

3/2
-Lévy superdiffusion with an interface that corresponds to the
location of the thermostat. The behavior of the process at the interface can be described
as follows: when the particle tries to jump over the heat bath,
it is either absorbed,
transmitted, or reflected with explicitly computable probabilities. In addition,
particles are created at the interface at the rates
depending on the bath temperature.

The direct derivation of the hydrodynamic limit
from the microscopic dynamics, in the presence of one or several heat baths at
different temperatures, has remained open.
To obtain such a limit is the main goal of the present paper.

Outline of the paper.

In Section
2
we
present the main results. In particular, Theorem
2.3

asserts the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions of equation (
1.6
). Theorem

2.4
concerns the regularity of the solution, provided
that the initial data in the equation is sufficiently regular. Our
main result concerning the
limit of average energy functional is formulated in Theorem
2.8
.
A key ingredient in the
proof is the fact that we can resolve the covariance matrix of the
stretch/momentum ensemble process
\big(\mathbf{r}(t),\mathbf{p}(t)\big)
, where
\mathbf{r}(t)=\big(r_{x}(t)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
\mathbf{p}(t)=\big(p_{x}(t)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
, obtained in Section
5
.

Section
3
is devoted to the discussion about the limiting
equation formulated in (
1.6
). In Section
3.2

we highlight the probabilistic interpretation of (
1.6
).

Preliminary to the proof of the main Theorem
2.8
,
we need to establish an
energy bound, formulated in Theorem
2.7
.
A simple argument using an identity satisfied by the covariances of
the field
\big(\mathbf{r}(t),\mathbf{p}(t)\big)
, see (
4.1
), under an additional assumption that

T_{L}=T_{R}
, is presented in Section
Supplement
.
We postpone the more technical argument, covering the case of
heat baths at different temperatures, till Sections
13

and
14
.

The energy bound of Theorem
2.7
. allows us to
conclude compactness of the energy distribution
in the
\star
-weak topology over
C[0,1]
. The limit identification is
conducted in Sections
6
–
11
.
In particular, the most delicate part of the proof is the
identification of the boundary layer, see
Theorem
9.1
, proven in Section
10
.
Section
12

is devoted to proving some technical results used throughout the
argument. In Section
A
of the Appendix we formulate some basic linear analysis
facts concerning the spectral resolution of the discrete Neumann
Laplacian and the gradient and divergence operators. Section

C
is devoted to finding the solution of equation
(
2.18
) with the prescribed boundary condition using the orthonormal
base of the Neumann Laplacian on
[0,1]
(cosine functions). In particular,
Section
C.4.2
identifies the unique stationary solution of
the equation, while Section
C.4.3
contains the proof of
Theorem
2.4
.
Section
B.2
is devoted to the proofs of the results appearing
in Section
3
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Dynamics in the stretch/momentum configuration space

Because of the translation invariance property of the dynamics, we only need
to consider the relative distance between the particles

r_{x}:=q_{x}-q_{x-1}=\nabla^{\star}q_{x}
,
x=0,\ldots,n
.
The configuration of particle stretches and momenta are described by

(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p})
as in (
1.3
).
The total energy of the chain is defined by the Hamiltonian:

(2.1)

\mathcal{H}_{n}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}):=\sum_{x=0}^{n}{\cal E}_{x}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}),

where the microscopic energy per particle is given by

(2.2)

{\cal E}_{x}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{p}):=\frac{1}{2}(p_{x}^{2}+r_{x}^{2}),\ \quad x=0,\dots,n,

with the convention that
r_{0}:=0
.

The microscopic dynamics of
the process
\{(\mathbf{r}(t),\mathbf{p}(t))\}_{t\geq 0}

describing the chain is given by:

(2.3)

\begin{split}&\dot{r}_{x}(t)=\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(t),\qquad x\in\{1,\dots,n\},\\
&\mathrm{d}p_{x}(t)=\nabla r_{x}\mathrm{d}t+\left[\nabla^{\star}p_{x+1}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{x,x+1}(\gamma t)-\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{x-1,x}(\gamma t)\right],\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{for }x=1,\ldots,n-1,\end{split}

and at the boundaries

(2.4)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}p_{0}(t)=r_{1}\mathrm{d}t+&\nabla^{\star}p_{1}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{0,1}(\gamma t)-\tilde{\gamma}p_{0}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\tilde{\gamma}T_{L}}\mathrm{d}w_{L}(t),\\
\\
\mathrm{d}p_{n}(t)=-r_{n}\mathrm{d}t&-\nabla^{\star}p_{n}(t-)\mathrm{d}N_{n-1,n}(\gamma t)-\tilde{\gamma}p_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\tilde{\gamma}T_{R}}\mathrm{d}w_{R}(t).\end{split}

The generator of the dynamics is given by

(2.5)

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}+\gamma S_{\text{ex}}+\tilde{\gamma}(S_{L}+S_{R}),

where, with the convention
r_{0}=r_{n+1}=0
, its Hamiltonian part equals

(2.6)

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\nabla^{\star}p_{x}\partial_{r_{x}}+\sum_{x=0}^{n}\nabla r_{x}\partial_{p_{x}},

the momentum exchange part is

(2.7)

\begin{split}&S_{\text{ex}}f({\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{p}})=\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\Big(f({\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{p}}^{x,x+1})-f({\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{p}})\Big).\end{split}

Here
f:\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded and measurable function,

{\mathbf{p}}^{x,x^{\prime}}
is the momentum configuration where the velocities at
sites
x\not=x^{\prime}
have been exchanged,
i.e.
{\mathbf{p}}^{x,x^{\prime}}=(p^{x,x^{\prime}}_{0},\ldots,p_{n}^{x,x^{\prime}})
, with
p_{y}^{x,x^{\prime}}=p_{y}
,

y\not\in\{x,x^{\prime}\}
and
p_{x^{\prime}}^{x,x^{\prime}}=p_{x}
,
p_{x}^{x,x^{\prime}}=p_{x^{\prime}}
. Finally, the
effects of
thermostats correspond to

(2.8)

S_{L}=T_{L}\partial_{p_{0}}^{2}-p_{0}\partial_{p_{0}}\quad S_{R}=T_{R}\partial_{p_{n}}^{2}-p_{n}\partial_{p_{n}}.

We assume that the initial distribution of stretches and momenta

(\mathbf{r}(0),\mathbf{p}(0))\in\Omega_{n}
is
random and distributed according to a probability measure

\mu_{n}
defined on the
\sigma
-algebra
{\cal B}_{n}
of Borel subsets of
the configuration space. Denote by
\mathbb{P}_{n}=\mu_{n}\otimes\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}_{n}

the probability measure on the product space
\big(\Omega_{n}\times\Sigma,{\cal B}_{n}\otimes{\cal F}\big)
and its
corresponding expectation. We decompose the configurations

(2.9)

r_{x}(t):=r_{x}^{\prime}(t)+\bar{r}_{x}(t),\qquad p_{x}(t):=p_{x}^{\prime}(t)+\bar{p}_{x}(t),

where the configuration of the means

(2.10)

\begin{split}\bar{\mathbf{r}}(t)&=(\bar{r}_{1}(t),\ldots,\bar{r}_{n}(t)):=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\mathbf{r}(t)],\\
\bar{\mathbf{p}}(t)&=(\bar{p}_{0}(t),\ldots,\bar{p}_{n}(t)):=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\mathbf{p}(t)],\end{split}

while
{\mathbf{r}}^{\prime}(t),{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}(t)
corresponds to the
fluctuating parts
of the dynamics.
It turns out that in the scaling we are concerned with the limiting
behavior of the system is not affected by the dynamics of the
means. For this reason and also to simplify the presentation we adopt
the following.

Assumption 2.1
.

We assume that

(2.11)

\bar{\mathbf{r}}(0)\equiv 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bar{\mathbf{p}}(0)\equiv 0.

This assumption obviously implies that
\bar{\mathbf{r}}(t)\equiv 0

and
\bar{\mathbf{p}}(t)\equiv 0
for all
t\geq 0
.

2.2.
Fractional diffusion equation with boundary conditions

Let

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{\rm N}[0,1]:=\big\{\varphi\in C^{\infty}[0,1]:\,\varphi^{\prime}(0)=\varphi^{\prime}(1)=0\big\}\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c}(0,1):=\big\{\varphi\in C^{\infty}[0,1]:\,{\rm supp}\,\varphi\in(0,1)\big\}.

Define the Neumann Laplacian
\Delta_{N}:C^{\infty}_{\rm N}[0,1]\to L^{2}[0,1]
as the closure of the operator

(2.12)

\Delta_{N}\varphi(u)=\varphi^{\prime\prime}(u),\quad\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\rm N}[0,1],\,u\in[0,1].

Using the spectral decomposition of the Laplacian in the orthonormal
base given by the cosine functions, see Section
C.1
, we can
define a self-adjoint operator
|\Delta_{N}|^{3/4}:{\cal D}(|\Delta_{N}|^{3/4})\to L^{2}[0,1]
, see (
C.1
), and the
respective Sobolev spaces
H^{3/4}[0,1]
,
H^{3/4}_{0}[0,1]
, see Section
C.1.1
.

For
\varrho>0
define the resolvent operator

(2.13)

G_{\varrho}[\varphi](u):=(\varrho-\Delta_{N})^{-1}\varphi(u)=\int_{0}^{1}G_{\varrho}(u,v)\varphi(v)\mathrm{d}v,

with the Green’s function
G_{\varrho}(u,v)
given by formula
(
B.2
).

Denote

(2.14)

V_{\varrho}(u,v):=\varrho G_{\varrho}(u,v),\quad\varrho>0,\,u,v\in[0,1].

By applying (
2.13
) to
\varphi=1
it follows that

(2.15)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u=1,\quad v\in[0,1],

and by (
B.2
) that
V_{\varrho}(u,v)\geq 0
.
Furthermore, see Lemma
C.4
,
for
\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(0,1)
we have

(2.16)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(u)V_{\varrho}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\Big)^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}<+\infty,\quad v=0,1.

This bound is equivalent with

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(u)G_{\varrho^{4/9}}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\Big)^{2}\mathrm{d}\varrho<+\infty,

Definition 2.2
.

Suppose that
c_{\rm bulk},c_{\rm bd}>0
,
T_{0},T_{1}>0
and
T_{\rm ini}\in L^{2}[0,1]
. Assume furthermore that
\tilde{\gamma}>0
and

(2.17)

\displaystyle F(\tilde{\gamma},\varrho):=\frac{\sinh\varrho+2\widetilde{\gamma}\cosh\varrho}{(1+4\widetilde{\gamma}^{2})\sinh\varrho+4\widetilde{\gamma}\cosh\varrho}

\displaystyle G(\tilde{\gamma},\varrho):=\frac{2\widetilde{\gamma}}{(1+4\widetilde{\gamma}^{2})\sinh\varrho+4\widetilde{\gamma}\cosh\varrho},\quad\varrho\geq 0.

We say that a
function
T:[0,+\infty)\to L^{2}[0,1]

is a weak solution of

(2.18)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}T(t,u)&=-c_{\rm bulk}|\Delta|^{3/4}T(t,u)\\
&+c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big\{{V_{\varrho}(u,v)}\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\Big\}\frac{F(\tilde{\gamma},\varrho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\\
&-c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big\{{V_{\varrho}(u,1-v)}\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\Big\}\frac{G(\tilde{\gamma},\varrho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}},\end{split}

with the boundary
values
T(t,v)=T_{v}
,
v=0,1
,
if the following hold:

i)

T\in C\Big([0,+\infty);L^{2}_{w}[0,1]\Big)
, where

L^{2}_{w}[0,1]
is equipped with the weak topology,

ii)

for any
t>0
and
v=0,1
we have

(2.19)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)\big(T_{v}-T(s,u^{\prime})\big)\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\Big)^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}<+\infty,

iii)

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,1)
we have:

(2.20)

\begin{split}&\langle\varphi,T(t)\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}-\langle\varphi,T_{\rm ini}\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}=-c_{\rm bulk}\int_{0}^{t}\langle|\Delta|^{3/4}\varphi,T(s)\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{+\infty}\langle V_{\varrho}(\cdot,v),\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}\langle V_{\varrho}(\cdot,v),T_{v}-T(s)\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}\frac{F(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\\
&-c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big\{\langle V_{\varrho}(\cdot,1-v),\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}[0,1]}\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(s,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\Big\}\frac{G(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}.\end{split}

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that
T_{\rm ini}\in L^{2}[0,1]
. Then, equation
(
2.18
) has a unique solution
T(\cdot,\cdot)
. In addition, the
solution satisfies

(2.21)

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}T(s,\cdot)\mathrm{d}s\in C[0,1]\quad\mbox{and}\\
&\int_{0}^{t}T(s,0)\mathrm{d}s={T_{0}}t,\quad\int_{0}^{t}T(s,1)\mathrm{d}s={T_{1}}t,\quad t\geq 0.\end{split}

The proof of Theorem
2.3
is presented in Section
C.2
of the Appendix.
In fact the results contained there allow us
to claim some additional regularity of solutions of
(
2.18
). For this purpose we consider the fractional Sobolev
space
H^{3/4}[0,1]
introduced in Section
C.1
. In particular,
H^{3/4}[0,1]\subset C[0,1]
, see Lemma
C.1
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that
T_{\rm ini}\in H^{3/4}[0,1]
is such that
T_{\rm ini}(v)=T_{v}
,
v=0,1
. Then:

i)

the solution
T(t)
of
(
2.18
) belongs to the space

C\Big([0,+\infty);H^{3/4}[0,1]\Big)

, where the target space
is considered with the strong topology,

ii)

we have

(2.22)

T_{0}=T(t,0)\quad\mbox{and}\quad T_{1}=T(t,1),\quad\mbox{for
all }t\geq 0.

The proof of the result is presented in Section
C.4.3
of the Appendix.

2.3.
Scaled dynamics of the chain

From now on we consider the process in the macroscopic time, i.e.

\big(\mathbf{r}_{n}(t),\mathbf{p}_{n}(t))\big)=\big(\mathbf{r}(n^{3/2}t),\mathbf{p}(n^{3/2}t))\big),

t\geq 0
.
The generator of the dynamics is given by
n^{3/2}\mathcal{G}
, where

\mathcal{G}
is defined in (
2.5
).
Since the time scale is fixed we will drop the index
n
from the notations
for the configurations at macroscopic time
t
.

Denote by
\mu_{n}(t)
the probability measure that is the distribution
of the configuration
({\mathbf{r}}(t),{\mathbf{p}}(t))
on
\Omega_{n}
.
Recall that thanks to Assumption
2.1
,
we have only the fluctuation part of the dynamics.

2.4.
The macroscopic limit of the energy functional

We assume the existence of the initial macroscopic energy profile.

Assumption 2.5
.

Assume that there exists a
function (the initial temperature profile)

T_{\rm ini}:[0,1]\to(0,+\infty)
such that, for any
\varphi\in C[0,1]
,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\varphi\left(\frac{x}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[{\cal E}_{n,x}(0)\big]=\int_{0}^{1}T_{\rm ini}(u)\varphi(u)\mathrm{d}u.

We suppose furthermore that
T_{\rm ini}\in H^{3/4}[0,1]
.

Let

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{x,x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{x}(t)p_{x^{\prime}}(t)\right]^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[r_{x}(t)r_{x^{\prime}}(t)\right]^{2}+2\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{x}(t)r_{x^{\prime}}(t)\right]^{2}\bigg\}.

Assumption 2.6
.

We assume that there exists
C_{2,{\cal H}}>0
such that

(2.25)

{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(0)\leq C_{2,{\cal H}}.

Let

(2.26)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{n}(t)=\sum_{x=0}^{n}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{x}^{2}(t)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[r_{x}^{2}(t)\right]\bigg\}.

As a consequence of the above assumptions we conclude
the energy bound.

Theorem 2.7
.

Under Assumptions
2.5
and
2.6
, for any
t_{*}>0
there exists
C_{\mathcal{H},t_{*}}>0
such that

(2.27)

\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[\mathcal{H}_{n}(t)\big]\leq C_{\mathcal{H},t_{*}}n,\qquad t\in[0,t_{*}],\,n=1,2,\ldots.

The proof of this result in the case
T_{L}=T_{R}
is presented in
Section
4
. The general case is shown in Section
13
.

Theorem 2.8

(The limit of thermal energy and equipartition)
.

Under the assumptions made in the present section for any continuous test function
\varphi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and any
t\geq 0
, we have

(2.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\varphi\left(\frac{x}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[{\cal E}_{n,x}(t)\big]=\int_{0}^{1}T(t,u)\varphi(u)\mathrm{d}u,

where
T(t,u)
is the solution of (
2.18
) with the initial data

T(0,u)=T_{\rm ini}(u)
and the boundary conditions
T(t,0)=T_{L}
,

T(t,1)=T_{R}
. Here

(2.29)

\displaystyle c_{\rm bulk}=\frac{1}{(2^{3}\gamma)^{1/2}},

\displaystyle{c_{\rm bd}=\frac{\widetilde{\gamma}}{2\pi\gamma^{1/2}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\widetilde{\gamma}}{\pi}c_{\rm bulk}}.

In addition, for any compactly supported,
continuous function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}

(2.30)

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Phi\left(t,\frac{x}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[p_{x}^{2}(t)\big]\mathrm{d}t&=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Phi\left(t,\frac{x}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[{\cal E}_{x}(t)\big]\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\int_{0}^{1}T(t,u)\Phi\left(t,u\right)\mathrm{d}u.\end{split}

The proof of Theorem
2.8
is given in Section

7
(the
convergence part) and in Section
12.2
(the equipartition property).

2.5.
Energy currents

Recall that now the generator of the process is given by
n^{3/2}\mathcal{G}
.
Energy currents satisfy

(2.31)

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[{\cal E}_{x}(t)\right]=n^{3/2}\mathcal{G}\mathcal{E}_{x}(t)=-n^{3/2}\nabla^{\star}j_{x,x+1}^{(n)}(t),\quad x=0,\ldots,n,

with

(2.32)

\displaystyle j_{x,x+1}(t)=j_{x,x+1}^{(a)}(t)+j_{x,x+1}^{(s)},\quad\mbox{where}

\displaystyle j_{x,x+1}^{(a)}(t):=-p_{x}(t)r_{x+1}(t),\quad j_{x,x+1}^{(s)}=-\frac{\gamma}{2}\big(p_{x+1}^{2}-p_{x}^{2}\big),\qquad\mbox{for }\quad x=0,...,n,

and at the boundaries

(2.33)

{j_{-1,0}:=\tilde{\gamma}\left(T_{L}-p_{0}^{2}\right)},\qquad j_{n,n+1}:=\tilde{\gamma}\left(p_{n}^{2}-T_{R}\right).

By a direct calculation we obtain

(2.34)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbb{E}_{n}{\cal H}_{n}(t)=n^{3/2}\tilde{\gamma}\Big[T_{L}+T_{R}-\mathbb{E}_{n}\Big(p_{0}^{2}(t)+p_{n}^{2}(t)\Big)\Big].

Hence

(2.35)

\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{n}p_{0}^{2}(s)+\mathbb{E}_{n}p_{n}^{2}(s)\Big)\mathrm{d}s\leq(T_{L}+T_{R})t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}\mathbb{E}_{n}{\cal H}_{n}(0),

and, cf (
2.33
),

(2.36)

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[j_{-1,0}(s)-j_{n,n+1}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{C}{\tilde{\gamma}\sqrt{n}}.

Concerning the current size estimate we have the following.

Theorem 2.9
.

Under Assumptions
2.5
and
2.6
, for any
t_{*}>0
there exists
C_{\mathcal{J},t_{*}}>0

such that

(2.37)

\sup_{x=0,\ldots,n+2}|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[j_{x-1,x}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s|\leq\frac{C_{\mathcal{J},t_{*}}}{\sqrt{n}},\quad t\in[0,t_{*}],\,n=1,2,\ldots.

The proof of the theorem is presented in Section
13.3
.

3.
Some remarks about the limiting equation

3.1.
Discussion about
the coefficients of (
2.18
)

A direct calculation, using formula (
B.1
), shows that
for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}[0,1]
we have

(3.1)

\begin{split}-|\Delta|^{3/4}\varphi(u)=\int_{0}^{1}q(u^{\prime},u)[\varphi(u^{\prime})-\varphi(u)]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},\end{split}

with

(3.2)

\displaystyle q(u,u^{\prime}):=\frac{3}{2^{5/2}{\pi}^{1/2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\frac{1}{|u+u^{\prime}+2n|^{5/2}}+\frac{1}{|u-u^{\prime}+2n|^{5/2}}\Big).

Let

r:=\frac{1-2\widetilde{\gamma}}{1+2\widetilde{\gamma}}.

Since
\widetilde{\gamma}>0
, we have
|r|<1
.
From (
2.17
) we can write

(3.3)

\displaystyle F(\widetilde{\gamma},\varrho)=\frac{1}{1+2\widetilde{\gamma}}\cdot\frac{1-re^{-2\varrho}}{1-r^{2}e^{-2\varrho}}=\frac{1}{1+2\widetilde{\gamma}}\left[1+\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\big(r^{2m}-r^{2m-1}\big)e^{-2m\varrho}\right]

and

(3.4)

\displaystyle G(\widetilde{\gamma},\varrho)=\frac{4\widetilde{\gamma}}{(1+2\widetilde{\gamma})^{2}}\cdot\frac{e^{-\varrho}}{1-r^{2}e^{-2\varrho}}=\frac{4\widetilde{\gamma}}{(1+2\widetilde{\gamma})^{2}}\sum_{m=0}^{+\infty}r^{2m}e^{-(2m+1)\varrho}.

Let

(3.5)

\begin{split}&g_{F}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,v)V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)\frac{F(\tilde{\gamma},\sqrt{\varrho})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\quad\mbox{and}\\
&\\
&g_{G}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,1-v)V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)\frac{G(\tilde{\gamma},\sqrt{\varrho})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\end{split}

Recall that, for the Neumann resolvent,

V_{\varrho}(u,0)=\sqrt{\varrho}\frac{\cosh(\sqrt{\varrho}(1-u))}{\sinh\sqrt{\varrho}},\qquad V_{\varrho}(u,1)=\sqrt{\varrho}\frac{\cosh(\sqrt{\varrho}u)}{\sinh\sqrt{\varrho}}.

Substituting into the definition of
g_{F}(u,u^{\prime};v)
and subsequently using the
expansion (
3.3
) we get

(3.6)

\begin{split}&g_{F}(u,u^{\prime};v)=\frac{1}{1+2\tilde{\gamma}}\,g_{0}(u,u^{\prime};v)+\frac{1}{1+2\tilde{\gamma}}\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\big(r^{2m}-r^{2m-1}\big)g_{m}(u,u^{\prime};v),\\
&g_{G}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)=\frac{4\tilde{\gamma}}{(1+2\tilde{\gamma})^{2}}\Big[g_{0}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)+\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}r^{2m}g_{m}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)\Big].\end{split}

Here

(3.7)

\begin{split}&g_{m}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,v)V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)e^{-2m\sqrt{\varrho}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}},\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots,\\
&g_{m}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,1-v)V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)e^{-(2m+1)\sqrt{\varrho}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}},\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots.\end{split}

Obviously
g_{m+1}(u,u^{\prime};v)\leq g_{m}(u,u^{\prime};v)
and
g_{m+1}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)\leq g_{m}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)
,
m=0,1,2,\ldots

In Appendix Section
B.2
, we show that

(3.8)

g_{m}(u,u^{\prime};v)=\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\sum_{n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\bigl(2m+|u+v+2n|+|u^{\prime}+v+2n^{\prime}|\bigr)^{5/2}}

and

(3.9)

\displaystyle g_{m}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v)={}

\displaystyle\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\sum_{n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\bigl(2m+1+|u+1-v+2n|+|u^{\prime}+v+2n^{\prime}|\bigr)^{5/2}}.

In the particular case,
\tilde{\gamma}=1/2

from
(
3.6
) we get

\begin{split}&g_{F}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\frac{1}{2}g_{0}(u,u^{\prime};v)\quad\mbox{and}\quad g_{G}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v):=\frac{1}{2}g_{0}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v).\end{split}

3.2.
Probabilistic interpretation

Suppose that
c_{\rm bulk}

and
c_{\rm bd}
are as in (
2.29
). Define

(3.10)

r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime}):=c_{\rm bulk}q(u,u^{\prime})-c_{\rm bd}\sum_{v=0,1}h_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime};v),

where

(3.11)

\displaystyle h_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime};v):=g_{F}(u,u^{\prime};v)-g_{G}^{\rm cr}(u,u^{\prime};v).

Equation (
2.18
) can be rewritten in the form

(3.12)

\partial_{t}T(t,u)=\int_{0}^{1}r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})\big[T(t,u^{\prime})-T(t,u)\big]\,\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}+\sum_{v=0,1}b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u;v)\big[T_{v}-T(t,u)\big],

where
r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})
is given by (
3.10
) and

(3.13)

b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u;v):=c_{\rm bd}\int_{0}^{1}h_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime};v)\,\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

If
r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})\geq 0
we can interpret (
1.5
) as the
equation describing the evolution of the density
T(t,u)
of
a Markov process
with creation and annihilation. The dynamics of the
process can be described as follows:
a particle
jumps from
u
to
u^{\prime}
with rate
r(u,u^{\prime})
(this takes into account the
jumps with reflection of the fractional Laplacian minus
the jumps censored by the boundaries).
At time
t
and position
u
the particle gets annihilated with rate

\big(b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,0)+b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,1)\big)

and it is created at this site at
rate
b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,0)T_{L}+b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,1)T_{R}
. The fact that the effective jump kernel is positive is
established in the following.

Proposition 3.1
.

If
c_{\rm bulk}

and
c_{\rm bd}
are given by (
2.29
), then

(3.14)

r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})\geq 0,\qquad u,u^{\prime}\in(0,1),\quad u\neq u^{\prime}.

More precisely,

(3.15)

r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{3/4}G_{\rho}^{(\widetilde{\gamma})}(u,u^{\prime})\,d\rho,

where
G_{\rho}^{(\widetilde{\gamma})}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Green’s function of

\rho-\frac{d^{2}}{du^{2}}

on
[0,1]
, subject to the Robin boundary conditions

(3.16)

f^{\prime}(0)=2\widetilde{\gamma}\sqrt{\rho}\,f(0),\qquad f^{\prime}(1)=-2\widetilde{\gamma}\sqrt{\rho}\,f(1).

Remark 3.2
.

More explicit formula for the kernel

r_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u,u^{\prime})
is given in (
B.25
).

The boundary behavior of the creation/absorption term is described in
the following.

Proposition 3.3
.

We have

(3.17)

b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u;v)\geq 0,\qquad u\in(0,1),\quad v\in\{0,1\}.

In addition,

(3.18)

b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u;v)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}c_{\rm bd}}{1+2\widetilde{\gamma}}|u-v|^{-3/2}+O(1),\qquad u\to v,\quad v\in\{0,1\}.

The term
O(1)
is uniformly bounded.
In particular,

\lim_{u\to v}b_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(u;v)=+\infty.

The proofs of the above results are presented in Sections
B.5

and
B.6
of
the Appendix.

4.
An identity for covariances and proof of the energy bound in the
case
T_{L}=T_{R}

4.1.
An identity for some covariances

After a tedious but direct calculation
we obtain the following identity, cf (
2.24
):

Proposition 4.1
.

For any
t\geq 0
and
n=2,3,\ldots
we have

(4.1)

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)+\frac{2\gamma n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\mathop{\sum_{x^{\prime}=0}^{n}}_{x^{\prime}\not\in\{x-1,x\}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(s)p_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\nabla\mathbb{E}_{n}p_{x}^{2}(s)\Big]^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{2\gamma n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sum_{x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(s)r_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\{T_{L}-\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p^{2}_{0}(s)\right]\Big\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\{T_{R}-\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p^{2}_{n}(s)\right]\Big\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{0}(s)p_{x}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{n}(s)p_{x}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{0}(s)r_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{n}(s)r_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle={\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(0)+\frac{2\tilde{\gamma}n^{3/2}}{n+1}\int_{0}^{t}\left[T_{L}\Big(T_{L}-\mathbb{E}_{n}p^{2}_{0}(s)\Big)+T_{R}\Big(T_{R}-\mathbb{E}_{n}p_{n}^{2}(s)\Big)\right]\mathrm{d}s.

The proof of identity (
4.1
) can be found in

[
15
, Section 1 of the Supplement]
.
We have the following result.

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose that
T_{L},T_{R}>0
. Then, for any
t_{*}>0
there exists
C>0
such that

(4.2)

{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)\leq C

for all
t\in[0,t_{*}]
and
n=2,3,\ldots
. Furthermore

(4.3)

\begin{split}&\sum_{x=1}^{n}\mathop{\sum_{x^{\prime}=0}^{n}}_{x^{\prime}\not\in\{x-1,x\}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(s)p_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{x=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\nabla^{\star}\mathbb{E}_{n}p_{x}^{2}(s)\Big]^{2}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sum_{x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(s)r_{x^{\prime}}(s)\right]\right\}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{z=0,n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[p_{z}(s)p_{x}(s)\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\sum_{z=0,n}\sum_{x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}[p_{z}(s)r_{x}(s)]^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/2}}.\end{split}

The proof of the theorem in the case
T_{L}=T_{R}
is presented in Section

Supplement
. The general case is treated in Section
14.7
.

4.2.
Proof of Theorems
2.7
and
4.2
in the case
T_{L}=T_{R}

We use (
4.1
) to prove Theorems
2.7

and
4.2
in the
case
T_{L}=T_{R}=T
. Then, from (
2.34
) and (
4.1
) we
conclude in particular that

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)+\frac{T}{n+1}{\cal H}_{n}(0)\leq{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(0)+\frac{T}{n+1}{\cal H}_{n}(t).

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
we have

(4.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1}{\cal H}_{n}(t)\leq\Big(2{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)\Big)^{1/2},

therefore

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)\leq{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(0)+\Big(2{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)\Big)^{1/2}-\frac{n^{3/2}T}{n+1}{\cal H}_{n}(0),

which in light of Assumptions
2.5
and
2.6

implies estimate (
4.2
). By virtue of (
4.4
) we
infer Theorem
2.7
. Using again (
4.1
)
we conclude also estimate (
4.3
).∎

5.
Covariance matrix

5.1.
Preliminaries

The stochastic evolution equation at macroscopic time are given by

(5.1)

\begin{split}\dot{r}_{x}(t)&=n^{3/2}\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(t),\quad\mbox{for }x=1,\dots,n,\\
\mathrm{d}{p}_{x}(t)&=n^{3/2}\Big(\nabla r_{x}+\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}p_{x}(t)\Big)\mathrm{d}t+\Big[\nabla^{\star}p_{x+1}(t-)\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{x,x+1}(\gamma t)\\
&-\nabla^{\star}p_{x}(t-)\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{x-1,x}(\gamma t)\Big],\quad\mbox{for }x=1,\dots,n-1.\end{split}

Here
\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{x-1,x}(t):=N^{(n)}_{x-1,x}(t)-n^{3/2}t
are independent zero mean martingales.
At the boundaries we have

(5.2)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}p_{0}(t)=n^{3/2}\Big(\nabla r_{0}&+\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}p_{0}(t)\Big)\mathrm{d}t+\nabla^{\star}p_{1}(t-)\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}_{0,1}^{(n)}(\gamma t)\\
&-n^{3/2}\tilde{\gamma}p_{0}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2n^{3/2}\tilde{\gamma}T_{L}}\mathrm{d}w_{L}(t),\\
\mathrm{d}p_{n}(t)=n^{3/2}\Big(\nabla r_{n}&+\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}p_{n}(t)\Big)\mathrm{d}t-\nabla^{\star}p_{n}(t-)\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{n-1,n}(\gamma t)\\
&-n^{3/2}\tilde{\gamma}p_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2n^{3/2}\tilde{\gamma}T_{R}}\mathrm{d}ew_{R}(t).\end{split}

Let

(5.3)

{\bf X}(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\bf r}(t)\\
{\bf p}(t)\end{array}\right).

The solution of (
5.1
)–
(
2.4
) satisfies

(5.4)

{\bf X}(t)=e^{-n^{3/2}At}{\bf X}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-n^{3/2}A(t-s)}\Sigma\Big({\bf p}(s-)\Big)\mathrm{d}M_{n}(s),\quad t\geq 0.

Here
A
is a
2\times 2
block matrix of the form

(5.5)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0_{n}&-\nabla^{\star}\\
-\nabla&-\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}+\tilde{\gamma}E\end{array}\right),

where
E=[\delta_{x,0}\delta_{y,0}+\delta_{x,n}\delta_{y,n}]_{x,y=0,\ldots,n}

and

0_{n,m}
is the
n\times m
null matrix. We use the shorthand
notation
0_{n}=0_{n,n}
. Here also
M_{n}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}M_{n}(s)
is
a
2n+2
-dimensional, zero mean vector martingale, where

\mathrm{d}M(s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0_{n,1}\\
n^{3/4}\mathrm{d}w_{L}(s)\\
\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{0,1}(\gamma s)\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}^{(n)}_{n-1,n}(\gamma s)\\
n^{3/4}\mathrm{d}w_{R}(s)\end{array}\right).

Its covariation matrix is
a block matrix of the form
n^{3/2}\Sigma({\bf p})
, where

(5.6)

\begin{split}\Sigma({\bf p})=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0_{n,n}&0_{n,n+2}\\
0_{n+1,n}&D({\bf p})\end{array}\right].\end{split}

Here

D({\bf p})
is an
(n+1)\times(n+2)
-dimensional matrix, given by

(5.7)

\begin{split}D({\bf p})=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{2\tilde{\gamma}T_{L}}&\nabla^{\star}p_{1}&0&\dots&0&0&0\\
0&-\nabla^{\star}p_{1}&\nabla^{\star}p_{2}&\dots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-\nabla^{\star}p_{2}&\dots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\dots&\nabla^{\star}p_{n-1}&0&0\\
0&0&0&\dots&-\nabla^{\star}p_{n-1}&\nabla^{\star}p_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&\dots&0&-\nabla^{\star}p_{n}&\sqrt{2\tilde{\gamma}T_{R}}\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

Denote by
S(t)
the
the covariance matrix

(5.8)

S(t)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{n}}\Big[{\bf X}_{n}(t)\otimes{\bf X}_{n}(t)\Big]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{S^{(r)}(t)}&S^{(r,p)}(t)\\
S^{(p,r)}(t)&S^{(p)}(t)\end{array}\right],

where

\displaystyle S^{(r)}(t)=\Big[\mathbb{E}_{n}[r_{x}(t)r_{y}(t)]\Big]_{x,y=1,\ldots,n},\quad S^{(r,p)}(t)=\Big[\mathbb{E}_{n}[r_{x}(t)p_{y}(t)]\Big]_{x=1,\ldots,n,y=0,\ldots,n},

(5.9)

\displaystyle S^{(p)}(t)=\Big[\mathbb{E}_{n}[p_{x}(t)p_{y}(t)]\Big]_{x,y=0,\ldots,n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad S^{(p,r)}(t)=\Big[S^{(r,p)}(t)\Big]^{T}.

Furthermore for a vector
{\bf x}=[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
we let

(5.10)

\begin{split}\Sigma_{2}({\bf x})=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0_{n,n}&0_{n,n+1}\\
0_{n+1,n}&\gamma D_{2}({\bf x})+2\tilde{\gamma}D_{1}\end{array}\right].\end{split}

Here

D_{1}=[T_{L}\delta_{x,0}\delta_{0,y}+T_{R}\delta_{x,n}\delta_{0,n}]_{x,y=0,\ldots,n}
and

(5.11)

\begin{split}&D_{2}({\bf x})\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}&-x_{1}&0&\dots&0&0&0\\
-x_{1}&x_{1}+x_{2}&-x_{2}&\dots&0&0&0\\
0&-x_{2}&x_{2}+x_{3}&\dots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\dots&x_{n-2}+x_{n-1}&-x_{n-1}&0\\
0&0&0&\dots&-x_{n-1}&x_{n-1}+x_{n}&-x_{n}\\
0&0&0&\dots&0&-x_{n}&x_{n}\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

From (
5.4
) we obtain

\displaystyle S(t)=\mathbb{E}_{n}\Big[e^{-An^{3/2}t}{\bf X}(0)\otimes{\bf X}(0)e^{-A^{T}n^{3/2}t}\Big]

\displaystyle+n^{3/2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-An^{3/2}(t-s)}\Sigma_{2}\Big(\overline{\mathfrak{\bf(\nabla p)}^{2}}(s)\Big)e^{-A^{T}n^{3/2}(t-s)}\mathrm{d}s

where
A
is given by (
5.5
)
and

\overline{\mathfrak{\bf(\nabla p)}^{2}}(s)=\Big[\mathbb{E}_{n}\big(\nabla^{\star}p_{1}(s)\big)^{2},\ldots,\mathbb{E}_{n}\big(\nabla^{\star}p_{n}(s)\big)^{2}\Big].

Consequently

(5.12)

A\big\langle\!\big\langle S\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}+\big\langle\!\big\langle S\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}A^{T}-\Sigma_{2}\Big(\big\langle\!\big\langle\overline{\mathfrak{\bf(\nabla p)}^{2}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big)=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}S,

where for a given stochastic process
\big(f(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
, taking values
in an appropriate space, we let

(5.13)

\big\langle\!\big\langle f\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\delta_{0,t}f:=\mathbb{E}_{n}f(0)-\mathbb{E}_{n}f(t).

5.2.
Resolution of the covariance matrix

Equation (
5.12
) leads to the following equations on the blocks

(5.14)

\displaystyle\Big[\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p,r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big]^{T}=\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r,p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},

\displaystyle\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r,p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\nabla-\nabla^{\star}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p,r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}S^{(r)},

\displaystyle-\nabla\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}-\Big(\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}-\tilde{\gamma}E\Big)\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p,r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}+\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\nabla=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}S^{(p,r)},

\displaystyle\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\nabla^{\star}-\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r,p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big(\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}-\tilde{\gamma}E\Big)-\nabla^{\star}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}S^{(r,p)},

(5.15)

\displaystyle-\nabla\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r,p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}+\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p,r)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\nabla^{\star}=\gamma D_{2}\Big(\big\langle\!\big\langle\overline{\mathfrak{\bf(\nabla p)}^{2}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big)+2\tilde{\gamma}D_{1}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big(\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}-\tilde{\gamma}E\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\Big(\gamma\Delta_{\rm N}-\tilde{\gamma}E\Big)\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p)}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}+\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}S^{(p)}.

To solve the system it is convenient to work with the Fourier
transforms of the matrices. Let
\psi_{0}(x),\ldots,\psi_{n}(x)
and
\phi_{1}(x),\ldots,\phi_{n}(x)
be
the respective orthonormal bases of the Neumann and Dirichlet
discrete Laplacians defined in (
A.1
). Define the Fourier
transforms of the stretch and momenta by

(5.16)

\tilde{r}_{j}(t):=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\phi_{j}(x)r_{x}(t)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{p}_{j}(t):=\sum_{x=0}^{n}\psi_{j}(x)p_{x}(t).

Denote

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{(r,p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sum_{x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r,p)}_{x,x^{\prime}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\phi_{j}(x)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{r}_{j}\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{(p,r)}_{j^{\prime},j}=\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sum_{x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p,r)}_{x^{\prime},x}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\phi_{j}(x)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{r}_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{p}_{j}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{(r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(r)}_{x,x^{\prime}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\phi_{j}(x)\phi_{j^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{r}_{j}\tilde{r}_{j^{\prime}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{(p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(p)}_{x^{\prime},x}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\psi_{\ell}(x)\psi_{\ell^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{p}_{j}\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}

for
j,j^{\prime}=1,\ldots,n
.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,j^{\prime}}:=\gamma\sum_{y=0}^{n}\psi_{j}(y)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(y)\Big[\big\langle\!\big\langle(\nabla^{\star}p_{y})^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}+\big\langle\!\big\langle(\nabla^{\star}p_{y+1})^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\Big]

\displaystyle-\gamma\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big[\psi_{j}(y-1)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(y)+\psi_{j}(y)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(y-1)\big]\big\langle\!\big\langle(\nabla^{\star}p_{y})^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},\quad j,j^{\prime}=0,\ldots,n.

Due to the convention
p_{-1}=p_{0}
and
p_{n+1}=p_{n}
we have

\nabla^{\star}p_{0}=\nabla^{\star}p_{n+1}=0
and a simple calculation
(see
[
15
, Section 2 of the Supplement]
)
shows that

(5.17)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{j,j^{\prime}}=\gamma\gamma_{j}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\phi_{j}(y)\phi_{j^{\prime}}(y)\big\langle\!\big\langle(\nabla^{\star}p_{y})^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}.

Here
\lambda_{j}=\gamma_{j}^{2}
, where
\lambda_{j}
,
j=0,\ldots,n
are the
eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{\rm N}
, see (
A.1
).
We also let

(5.18)

\begin{split}&R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota)}=\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}\delta_{0,t}{\tilde{S}}_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota)},\quad\iota\in I:=\{p,pr,rp,r\}\quad\mbox{and}\\
\\
&B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(pr)}=\psi_{j}(0)\tilde{s}^{(p,\tilde{r})}_{0,j^{\prime}}+\psi_{j}(n)\tilde{s}^{(p,\tilde{r})}_{n,j^{\prime}},\quad B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(rp)}=B_{j^{\prime},j}^{(pr)},\\
&B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(p)}=2t\Big(T_{L}\psi_{j}(0)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(0)+T_{R}\psi_{j}(n)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(n)\Big)\\
&-\Big(\psi_{j}(0)\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{0,j^{\prime}}+\psi_{j}(n)\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{n,j^{\prime}}+\psi_{j^{\prime}}(0)\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{0,j}+\psi_{j^{\prime}}(n)\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{n,j}\Big),\end{split}

with

(5.19)

\displaystyle\tilde{s}^{(p,\tilde{r})}_{x,j}=\tilde{s}^{(\tilde{r},p)}_{j,x}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\psi_{\ell}(x)\tilde{S}^{(r,p)}_{j,\ell}=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{r}_{j}p_{x}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},

\displaystyle\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{x,j}=\tilde{s}^{(p)}_{j,x}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\psi_{\ell}(x)\tilde{S}^{(p)}_{j,\ell}=\big\langle\!\big\langle\tilde{p}_{j}p_{x}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}.

With the above notation
we can rewrite (
5.14
) for
all
j,j^{\prime}=0,\ldots,n
as follows

(5.20)

\begin{split}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{S}^{(r,p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma_{j}\tilde{S}^{(p,r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}&=R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(r)},\\
-\gamma_{j}\tilde{S}^{(r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma\lambda_{j}\tilde{S}^{(p,r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{S}^{(p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}&=R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(pr)}-\tilde{\gamma}B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(pr)},\\
-\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{S}^{(r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma\lambda_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{S}^{(r,p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma_{j}\tilde{S}^{(p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}&=R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(rp)}-\tilde{\gamma}B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(rp)},\\
-\gamma_{j}\tilde{S}^{(r,p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}-\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{S}^{(p,r)}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\gamma(\lambda_{j}+\lambda_{j^{\prime}})\tilde{S}^{(p)}_{j,j^{\prime}}&=R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(p)}+\tilde{\gamma}{B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(p)}}+\tilde{F}_{j,j^{\prime}}.\end{split}

Solving for
\tilde{S}^{(\iota)}_{j,j^{\prime}}
,
\iota=p,r,pr
(see

[
15
, Section 3 of the Supplement]
), we obtain

(5.21)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{(\iota)}_{j,j^{\prime}}=\Theta_{\iota}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})\tilde{F}_{j,j^{\prime}}+\sum_{\iota^{\prime}\in I}\Pi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota^{\prime})}+\sum_{\iota^{\prime}\in I}\Xi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota^{\prime})}.

Here

(5.22)

\begin{split}&\Theta_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=\frac{2\gamma cc^{\prime}}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})},\quad\mbox{where}\quad\theta(c,c^{\prime})=(c-c^{\prime})^{2}+2\gamma^{2}cc^{\prime}(c+c^{\prime}),\\
&\Theta_{r}(c,c^{\prime})=\frac{\gamma(c+c^{\prime})\sqrt{cc^{\prime}}}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})},\quad\Theta_{pr}(c,c^{\prime})=\frac{(c-c^{\prime})\sqrt{c^{\prime}}}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})}.\end{split}

The coefficients
\Xi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(c,c^{\prime})
are given by

(5.23)

\begin{split}&\Xi^{(\iota)}_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=\Theta_{\iota}(c,c^{\prime}),\quad\iota=p,pr,r,\\
&\Xi^{(p)}_{r}(c,c^{\prime})=-\Xi^{(pr)}_{rp}(c,c^{\prime})=\Theta_{r}(c,c^{\prime}),\\
&\Xi^{(p)}_{pr}(c,c^{\prime})=-\Theta_{pr}(c,c^{\prime}),\quad\Xi^{(p)}_{rp}(c,c^{\prime})=\Xi^{(p)}_{pr}(c^{\prime},c),\\
&\Xi^{(pr)}_{pr}(c,c^{\prime})=\frac{\gamma c^{\prime}(c+c^{\prime})}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})},\\
&\Xi^{(p,r)}_{r}(c,c^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{c}}\Big[1+\frac{c^{2}-(c^{\prime})^{2}}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})}\Big],\\
&\Xi^{(r)}_{rp}(c,c^{\prime})=\Xi^{(r)}_{pr}(c^{\prime},c)=-\Xi^{(pr)}_{r}(c,c^{\prime}),\\
&\Xi^{(r)}_{r}(c,c^{\prime})=\gamma\frac{c^{2}+(c^{\prime})^{2}+\gamma^{2}cc^{\prime}(c+c^{\prime})}{\theta(c,c^{\prime})}.\end{split}

Finally
\Pi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(c,c^{\prime})
are determined from

(5.24)

\begin{split}&\Pi^{(\iota)}_{p}(c,c^{\prime})=\tilde{\gamma}\Theta_{\iota}(c,c^{\prime}),\quad\iota=p,pr,r,\\
&\Pi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(c,c^{\prime})=-\tilde{\gamma}\Xi^{(\iota)}_{\iota^{\prime}}(c,c^{\prime}),\quad\iota=p,pr,r,\,\iota^{\prime}=pr,\,rp,\\
&\Pi^{(\iota)}_{r}(c,c^{\prime})=0,\quad\iota=p,pr,rp,r.\end{split}

5.3.
Further covariance bounds from (
4.1
)

Recall definition (
5.16
) of
\tilde{r}_{j},\tilde{p}_{j}
.

Corollary 5.1
.

For any
t_{*}>0
there exists
C>0
such that

(5.25)

\begin{split}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=0}^{n}\Big[\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{r}_{j}(t)\tilde{r}_{j^{\prime}}(t)]\big)^{2}&+\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{r}_{j}(t)\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}(t)]\big)^{2}\\
&+\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{p}_{j}(t)\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}(t)]\big)^{2}\Big]\leq C(n+1),\end{split}

and

(5.26)

\begin{split}&\sup_{j=0,\ldots,n}\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}\tilde{r}_{j}^{2}(t)+\mathbb{E}_{n}\tilde{p}_{j}^{2}(t)\big)\leq C(n+1)^{1/2},\\
&\sup_{j,j^{\prime}=0,\ldots,n}\big|\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[\tilde{r}_{j}(t)\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}(t)\big]\big|\leq C(n+1)^{1/2}\quad\mbox{ for $t\in[0,t_{*}]$, $n=1,2,\ldots$}.\end{split}

In addition, for
z=0,n
we have

(5.27)

\begin{split}&\sup_{j=1,\ldots,n}\big|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{r}_{j}(s)p_{z}(s)]\mathrm{d}s\big|\leq\frac{C}{(n+1)^{1/4}}\quad\mbox{and}\\
&\sup_{j=0,\ldots,n}\big|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{p}_{j}(s)p_{z}(s)]\mathrm{d}s\big|\leq\frac{C}{(n+1)^{1/4}}\quad\mbox{ for $t\in[0,t_{*}]$, $n=1,2,\ldots$}.\end{split}

Proof of (
5.25
)

We show that

(5.28)

\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{p}_{j}(t)\tilde{p}_{j^{\prime}}(t)]\big)^{2}\leq C(n+1),

the arguments in the other cases are similar.
The expression on the left hand side equals

\displaystyle\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=0}^{n}\sum_{x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}=0}^{n}\psi_{j}(x)\psi_{j}(x^{\prime})\psi_{j^{\prime}}(y)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})\mathbb{E}_{n}[p_{x}(t)p_{x^{\prime}}(t)]\mathbb{E}_{n}[p_{y}(t)p_{y^{\prime}}(t)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}[p_{x}(t)p_{x^{\prime}}(t)]\big)^{2}\leq C(n+1),

thus (
5.28
) follows by Theorem
4.2
.

Proof of (
5.26
)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{n}\tilde{p}_{j}^{2}(t)\leq 2\left\{\sum_{x,x^{\prime}=0}^{n}\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[p_{x}(t)p_{x^{\prime}}(t)\big]\big)^{2}\right\}^{1/2}

and the desired estimate is a consequence of Theorem

4.2
. The
proofs of the other estimates in (
5.26
) are analogous.∎

Proof of (
5.27
)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

\displaystyle\big|\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}[\tilde{r}_{j}(s)p_{z}(s)]\big|\mathrm{d}s\leq\left\{\sum_{x=1}^{n}\big\{\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}[r_{x}(s)p_{z}(s)]\mathrm{d}s\big\}^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{(n+1)^{1/4}}.

The desired estimate is a consequence of (
4.3
). The
proofs of the other estimates in (
5.27
) follow the same argument.
∎

6.
Limit identification. Formulation of the results

6.1.
Time evolution of the energy density

Consider a test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,1)
. Define

\varphi_{x}=\varphi(u_{x})
, where

(6.1)

u_{x}=\frac{x}{n+1},

and

(6.2)

{\rm E}_{n}(t;\varphi)=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\varphi_{x}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[{\cal E}_{x}(t)\right].

We have

(6.3)

\begin{split}{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi)-{\rm E}_{n}(0,\varphi)&=-\frac{n^{3/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{x}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla^{\star}j_{x,x+1}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\frac{n^{1/2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{n,x}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[j_{x,x+1}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Here
\varphi_{n,x}^{\prime}:=n(\varphi_{x+1}-\varphi_{x})
. Using the
energy bound (
2.27
) we can replace
\varphi_{n,x}^{\prime}
by

\varphi^{\prime}_{x}:=\varphi^{\prime}(u_{x})
at the expense of an
error of size
o_{n}(1)
. Separating the parts of the current due to the Hamiltonian and
stochastic parts of the dynamics we get, cf (
2.32
),

(6.4)

\displaystyle{\rm E}_{n}(t;\varphi)-{\rm E}_{n}(0;\varphi)=J_{n}(t;\varphi^{\prime})+J_{n}^{(s)}(t;\varphi^{\prime})+o_{n}(1),\quad\mbox{where}

\displaystyle J_{n}(t;\varphi^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\varphi^{\prime}_{x}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[j_{x-1,x}^{(a)}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\varphi^{\prime}_{x}\big\langle\!\big\langle S^{(pr)}_{x-1,x}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t},

(6.5)

\displaystyle J_{n}^{(s)}(t;\varphi^{\prime}):=-\frac{\gamma}{2\sqrt{n}}\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{x}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla p_{x}^{2}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{2n^{3/2}}\sum_{x=0}^{n-1}\varphi_{x}^{\prime\prime}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[p_{x}^{2}(s)\right]\mathrm{d}s+o_{n}(1),\quad\mbox{and }\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{x}:=\varphi^{\prime\prime}(u_{x}).

Because of the energy bound (
2.27
), the contribution of
J_{n}^{(s)}
is
negligible.

Using (
S3.13
) for
\iota=pr
, we can write

(6.6)

\begin{split}J_{n}(t;\varphi^{\prime})&=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}{\tilde{S}}^{(pr)}_{j^{\prime},j}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\phi_{j}(x)\psi_{j^{\prime}}(x-1)\varphi^{\prime}_{x}\\
&=-\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\iota\in I}\xi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\iota\in I}\pi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n).\end{split}

Here
I=\{p,pr,rp,r\}
and

(6.7)

\displaystyle\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{j^{\prime}}}\Theta_{pr}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})F_{j,j^{\prime}},

(6.8)

\displaystyle\pi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}\Pi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})B_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota)},

(6.9)

\displaystyle\xi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}\Xi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})R_{j,j^{\prime}}^{(\iota)},

(6.10)

\displaystyle{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}:=\sqrt{n}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\phi_{j^{\prime}}(x)\psi_{j}(x-1)\varphi^{\prime}(u_{x}),

(6.11)

\displaystyle F_{j,j^{\prime}}=\gamma\sum_{y=1}^{n}\phi_{j}(y)\phi_{j^{\prime}}(y)\big\langle\!\big\langle(\nabla^{\star}p_{y})^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}.

We refer to
\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)
,
\pi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)
and
\xi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)
as the bulk,
boundary and time-coboundary terms respectively.
Before formulating the result for each of them
we introduce some notation.
Denote by

(6.12)

\begin{split}&c_{0}(u):=1,\qquad c_{\ell}(u):=\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi\ell u),\\
&s_{\ell}(u):=\sqrt{2}\sin(\pi\ell u),\qquad\ell=1,2,\dots,\,u\in[0,1],\end{split}

the cosine and sine orthonormal bases in
L^{2}[0,1]
.
Given a function
\varphi\in L^{2}[0,1]
we denote

(6.13)

\hat{\varphi}_{c}(\ell):=\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(u)c_{\ell}(u)du,\quad\hat{\varphi}_{s}(\ell):=\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(u)s_{\ell}(u)du

its Fourier coefficients in the respective bases.

For
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
and
j=1,\dots,n
we define

(6.14)

\widehat{f_{n,o}}(j):=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=1}^{n}\sin(\pi ju_{x})f(u_{x}),\qquad\widehat{f_{n,e}}(j):=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\cos(\pi ju_{x})f(u_{x}),

and for
j=0

(6.15)

\widehat{f_{n,e}}(0):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{x=0}^{n}f(u_{x}).

Suppose that
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,1)
. By
[
16
, Lemma B.1]
, for any
k>0
we have for some constant
C>0
:

(6.16)

|\widehat{f_{n,\iota}}(j)|\leq\frac{C}{\chi_{n}^{k}(j)},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z},\,n=1,2,\ldots,\qquad\iota=o,e,

where
\chi_{n}
is
2n+2
-periodic extension of the function

\chi_{n}(j)=(1+j)\wedge(2n+2-j),\quad j=0,\ldots,2n+1.

In addition, if
\kappa\in(0,1)
, then there exists
C>0
such that

(6.17)

\sup_{|j|\leq n^{\kappa}}\big(|\widehat{f_{n,o}}(j)-\hat{f}_{s}(j)|+|\widehat{f_{n,e}}(j)-\hat{f}_{c}(j)|\big)\leq\frac{C}{n^{1-\kappa}}.

The following results deal with each of the terms appearing on the
right hand side of (
6.6
). In all of them we shall assume
that the test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
. In addition, both here and in what follows
o_{n}(1)
denotes an arbitarry term that
satisfies

(6.18)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}o_{n}(1)=0.

Proposition 6.1

(Asymptotics of the bulk term)
.

We have

(6.19)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)&={-}\frac{1}{(2^{3}\gamma)^{1/2}n}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\cal E_{y}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sum_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}c_{\ell}(u_{y})(\pi\ell)^{1/2}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})}_{s}(\ell)\\
&=\frac{1}{(2^{3}\gamma)^{1/2}n}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\cal E_{y}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sum_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}(\pi\ell)^{3/2}\hat{\varphi}_{c}(\ell)c_{\ell}(u_{y})+o_{n}(1)\\
&=\frac{1}{(2^{3}\gamma)^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm E}_{n}\left(s,|\Delta_{\rm N}|^{3/4}\varphi\right)\mathrm{d}s+o_{n}(1),\end{split}

as
n\to+\infty
.
The operator
|\Delta_{\rm N}|^{3/4}
is defined in (
C.1
).

Proposition 6.2

(Asymptotics of the boundary term)
.

For
n\to+\infty
we have

(6.20)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\iota\in I}\frac{1}{n}\pi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\frac{\widetilde{\gamma}}{2\pi\gamma^{1/2}}\sum_{v=0,1}\int_{0}^{+\infty}V_{\varrho}(u,v)\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\frac{F(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}\\
&+\frac{\widetilde{\gamma}}{2\pi\gamma^{1/2}}\sum_{v=0,1}V_{\varrho}(u,1-v)\int_{0}^{1}V_{\varrho}(u^{\prime},v)[T_{v}-T(t,u^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\,\frac{G(\tilde{\gamma},\rho^{1/2})d\rho}{\rho^{3/4}}+o_{n}(1),\end{split}

with
V_{\rho}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in (
2.14
) and

F(\cdot,\cdot)
,
G(\cdot,\cdot)
given by (
2.17
).

Proposition 6.3

(Negligible time–boundary terms)
.

For
n\to+\infty
we have

(6.21)

\sum_{\iota\in I}\frac{1}{n}\xi^{(pr)}_{\iota}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=o_{n}(1).

The proofs of the above results are presented in Sections

8
–
11
.

7.
Compactness and conclusion of the proof of Theorem
2.8

7.1.
Compactness

Consider the subset
\mathcal{M}_{+,E_{*}}([0,1])
of
\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,1])
- the space of all positive, finite
Borel measures on
[0,1]
-
consisting of measures with total mass less than or equal to
E_{*}
. It is
compact in the topology of weak convergence of measures. In addition, the topology is metrizable
when restricted to this set. As a consequence of Corollary
2.7
for any
t_{*}>0
the total energy is bounded by

C_{\mathcal{H},t_{*}}
(see (
2.27
)) and we have that
{\rm E}_{n}(\cdot)\in{\cal C}_{\mathcal{M}}[0,t_{*}]:=C\left([0,t_{*}],\mathcal{M}_{+,C_{\mathcal{H},t_{*}}}([0,1])\right)
, where the latter space
is endowed with the topology of the uniform convergence.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{+,E_{*}}([0,1])
is compact, in order to show that
\big({\rm E}_{n}(\cdot)\big)_{n\geq 1}

is compact,
we only need to control
its modulus of continuity in time for any

\varphi\in C[0,1]
, see e.g.
[
13
, p. 234]
.
This will be a consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition 7.1
.

For any
\varphi\in C[0,1]
we have

(7.1)

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq s,t\leq t_{*},|t-s|<\delta}\left|{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi)-{\rm E}_{n}(s,\varphi)\right|=0

Proof.

From (
6.3
)
one can infer that for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
the mean
energy field
{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi)
, see (
6.3
), can be decomposed as follows

{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi)={\rm E}_{n}^{\theta}(t,\varphi)+{\rm E}_{n}^{\pi}(t,\varphi)+{\rm E}_{n}^{\xi}(t,\varphi)+o_{n}(t),

where
{\rm E}_{n}^{\theta}(t,\varphi)
corresponds to the bulk terms,
{\rm E}_{n}^{\pi}(t,\varphi)
- to the boundary terms and
{\rm E}_{n}^{\xi}(t,\varphi)
- to the time co-boundary terms. They are considered in Sections
8
,
9

and
11
respectively. The term
o_{n}(t)
that
gathers the stochastic current
J_{n}^{(s)}(t;\varphi^{\prime})
and negligible
terms that arise from replacing
\varphi^{\prime}
by
\varphi_{n,x}^{\prime}
(see (
6.4
))
satisfies

(7.2)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,t_{*}]}|o_{n}(t)|=0,\quad\mbox{for any }t_{*}>0.

It follows from the analysis of the bulk and boundary term (see
Sections
8
and
9
)
that for any
t_{*}>0

there exists
C>0

such that

\left|{\rm E}_{n}^{(\iota)}(t,\varphi)-{\rm E}_{n}(s,\varphi)\right|\leq C(t-s),\quad\iota\in\{\theta,\pi\},\,s,t\in[0,t_{*}],\,n=1,2,\ldots.

As for the time co-boundary term, the argument of Section

11
shows that it is of order
o_{n}(t)
satisfying
(
7.2
).
This implies (
7.1
) for any test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
.
If
\varphi\in C[0,1]
, then we can approximate it by a sequence of

(\varphi_{N})_{N\geq 1}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
in the
L^{2}
sense, as
N\to+\infty
. Thanks to
(
4.2
) we conclude that

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\sup_{0\leq t\leq t_{*},n\geq 1}\left|{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi_{N})-{\rm E}_{n}(t,\varphi)\right|=0

and equality (
7.1
) follows as well.
∎

7.2.
Properties of limiting points of
\left({\rm E}^{(n)}(\cdot)\right)

As we have already pointed out in Section
7.1

the sequence

\left({\rm E}^{(n)}(\cdot)\right)\subset{\cal C}_{\mathcal{M}}[0,t_{*}]:=C\left([0,t_{*}],\mathcal{M}_{+,C_{\mathcal{H},t_{*}}}([0,1])\right)
is compact in the uniform convergence topology for
each
t_{*}>0
. Any limiting point
{\rm E}(\cdot)
is a continuous function
{\rm E}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,1])
. In fact, it follows directly from Theorem

4.2
that

(7.3)

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x=0}^{n}\big(\mathbb{E}_{n}\big[{\cal E}_{n,x}(t)\big]\big)^{2}\leq{\cal H}_{n}^{(2)}(t)\leq C,\quad n=1,2,\ldots.

Therefore
{\rm E}(\cdot)
is of the form
{\rm E}(t,\varphi)=\int_{0}^{1}T(t,u)\varphi(u)\mathrm{d}u
, where
T(t,\cdot)
is
a square integrable function w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure for
any
t>0
. In what follows we shall identify the measure valued
function
{\rm E}(\cdot)
with its density
T(\cdot)
. Let
L^{2}_{w}[0,1]

denote the space of all square integrable functions on
[0,1]
, equipped with the
weak topology.

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose that
T(\cdot)
is a limiting point of
\Big({\rm E}_{n}(\cdot)\Big)_{n\geq 1}
.
Then, under the assumptions made in Section
2.4
,
we have
T(\cdot)\in C\left([0,+\infty),L^{2}_{w}[0,1]\right)
. The functions
{\cal b}^{(v)}:[0,+\infty)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,

v=0,\,1
given by

(7.4)

\displaystyle{\cal b}^{(v)}(s,\varrho):=T_{v}-\int_{0}^{1}V_{\rho}(u,v)T(s,u)\mathrm{d}u,\quad s,\varrho>0,

satisfy

(7.5)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big[{\cal b}^{(v)}(s,\varrho)\big]^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\varrho}{\varrho^{3/4}}<+\infty,\quad t>0,\,v=0,1.

In addition, for each
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,1)
equation
(
2.20
) holds.

Proof.

The fact that
T(t,\cdot)
satisfies equation (
2.20
)
follows directly from equation (
6.4
) and Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
.
From (
7.3
) it follows that
\sup_{t\geq 0}\|T(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}[0,1]}<+\infty
, which combined with
(
2.20
) implies that
T(\cdot)\in C\left([0,+\infty),L^{2}_{w}[0,1]\right)
. Finally,
(
7.5
) follows from Corollary
9.2
.
∎

7.3.
The end of the proof of convergence of the energy functional

According to Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
and Theorem

7.2
any limiting
point for the sequence
\Big({\rm E}_{n}(\cdot)\Big)_{n\geq 1}\subset{\cal C}_{\mathcal{M}}[0,t_{*}]
is a weak solution to (
2.18
) in
the sense of Definition
2.2
. To complete the argument for
the convergence of
\big({\rm E}_{n}(\cdot)\big)_{n\geq 1}
it suffices to invoke Theorem
2.3
that
asserts the uniqueness of such solutions.

8.
The asymptotics of the bulk term. Proof of Proposition
6.1

We first compute
{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}
defined in (
6.10
). Denote

(8.1)

k_{j}:=\frac{j}{n+1}.

A direct calculation (see
[
15
, Section 4 of the Supplement]
) leads to the following formula.

Lemma 8.1
.

For any function
\varphi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
{\rm supp}\,\varphi\subset(0,1)
we have

(8.2)

\begin{split}{\cal W}_{j,j^{\prime}}=&-\Big(\frac{n}{2}\Big)^{1/2}\left(1-\frac{\delta_{0,j}}{2}\right)^{1/2}\cos\Big(\frac{\pi k_{j}}{2}\Big)\Big[\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})-\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j+j^{\prime})\Big]\\
&-\Big(\frac{n}{2}\Big)^{1/2}\sin\Big(\frac{\pi k_{j}}{2}\Big)\Big[\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,e}}(j+j^{\prime})-\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,e}}(j-j^{\prime})\Big].\end{split}

Substituting from (
8.2
) into (
6.7
) and recalling (
5.22
),
we have

(8.3)

\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=\theta_{pr}^{(o)}(\varphi^{\prime};n)+\theta_{pr}^{(e)}(\varphi^{\prime};n),

with

(8.4)

\theta_{pr}^{(o)}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\Big(\frac{n}{2}\Big)^{1/2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sin(\pi k_{j})\Big[\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})-\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j+j^{\prime})\Big]\frac{\lambda_{j^{\prime}}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}{\theta(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}F_{j,j^{\prime}},

and

(8.5)

\begin{split}\theta_{pr}^{(e)}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\Big(\frac{n}{2^{3}}\Big)^{1/2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\Big[\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,e}}(j+j^{\prime})-\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,e}}(j-j^{\prime})\Big]\frac{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{j^{\prime}}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}{\theta(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}F_{j,j^{\prime}}\\
\end{split}

By a symmetry argument, interchanging
the roles of indices
j
,
j^{\prime}
in (
8.5
), we have
\theta_{pr}^{(e)}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=0
.
Using the above and the parity
F_{-j,j^{\prime}}=F_{j,-j^{\prime}}=-F_{j,j^{\prime}}

we conclude that (see
[
15
, Section 5 of the Supplement]
for detailed calculation)

(8.6)

\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=\theta_{pr}^{(o)}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\left(\frac{n}{2^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\sin(\pi k_{j})\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})\frac{\lambda_{j^{\prime}}(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}{\theta(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j^{\prime}})}F_{j,j^{\prime}},

Recalling (
5.17
) we have

(8.7)

\begin{split}\theta_{pr}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=&-\gamma\Big(\frac{n}{2^{3}}\Big)^{1/2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\sin(\pi k_{j})\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})\\
&\times\frac{\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2}(\gamma_{j}^{2}-\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2})}{\theta(\gamma_{j}^{2},\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2})}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\phi_{j}(y)\phi_{j^{\prime}}(y)\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\\
=&-\gamma\Big(\frac{n}{2^{3}}\Big)^{1/2}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\sin(\pi k_{j})\frac{\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2}(\gamma_{j}^{2}-\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2})}{\theta(\gamma_{j}^{2},\gamma_{j^{\prime}}^{2})}\\
&\times\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})\frac{\cos(\pi u_{y}(j-j^{\prime}))-\cos(\pi u_{y}(j+j^{\prime}))}{n+1}\\
=:&\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)-\theta_{pr,+}(\varphi^{\prime};n)\end{split}

Using elementary trigonometric formulas we conclude that

(8.8)

\begin{split}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\frac{\gamma n^{1/2}}{2^{3/2}(n+1)}&\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\frac{\sin(\pi k_{j})\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi(k_{j}+k_{j^{\prime}})}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi(k_{j}-k_{j^{\prime}})}{2}\right)}{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi(k_{j}+k_{j^{\prime}})}{2}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi(k_{j}-k_{j^{\prime}})}{2}\right)+2^{3}\gamma^{2}\Gamma(k_{j},k_{j^{\prime}})}\\
&\times\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(j-j^{\prime})\cos(\pi u_{y}(j-j^{\prime}))\\
=-\frac{\gamma n^{1/2}}{2^{3/2}(n+1)}&\sum_{\ell,j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\frac{\sin(\pi k_{\ell+j^{\prime}})\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell+2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\right)}{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell+2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\right)+2^{3}\gamma^{2}\Gamma(k_{\ell+j^{\prime}},k_{j^{\prime}})}\\
&\times\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell),\end{split}

where

(8.9)

\Gamma(k_{j},k_{j^{\prime}})=\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j}}{2}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\left(\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j}}{2}\right)+\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\right).

Choose
\kappa\in(0,1)
. We further adjust the parameter later on.
Thanks to (
6.16
) we can consider only the terms
|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}

and we have

(8.10)

\begin{split}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)=-\frac{\gamma n^{1/2}}{2^{3/2}(n+1)}&\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\frac{\sin(\pi k_{\ell+j^{\prime}})\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell+2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\right)}{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell+2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\right)+2^{3}\gamma^{2}\Gamma(k_{\ell+j^{\prime}},k_{j^{\prime}})}\\
&\times\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1).\end{split}

Since
|k_{\ell}|=|\ell|/(n+1)\leq(n+1)^{\kappa-1}
we can use approximate
equalities

(8.11)

\displaystyle\sin\Big(\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\Big)\approx\frac{\pi\ell}{2(n+1)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad 2k_{j^{\prime}}+k_{\ell}\approx 2k_{j^{\prime}}.

Then we have

(8.12)

\begin{split}\Gamma(k_{j^{\prime}+\ell},k_{j^{\prime}})\approx 2\sin^{6}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\end{split}

and, as a result, obtain

(8.13)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)&=-\frac{\gamma}{2^{3/2}(n+1)^{3/2}}\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\frac{\sin(\pi k_{j^{\prime}})\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi k_{2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)}{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{2j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\pi\ell}{2n}\right)^{2}+2^{4}\gamma^{2}\sin^{6}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)}\\
&\times\frac{\pi k_{\ell}}{2}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1)\\
&=-\frac{\gamma}{2^{2}(n+1)^{3/2}}\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\pi\ell\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\sum_{j^{\prime}=-n-1}^{n}\frac{\sin^{2}\left(\pi k_{j^{\prime}}\right)}{\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\pi\ell}{n}\right)^{2}+2^{4}\gamma^{2}\sin^{4}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)}\\
&\times\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}2^{1/2}\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1)\\
\end{split}

Choose
\delta\in(0,1)
. Observe that if
|j^{\prime}|\geq\delta n
the denominator in the
last expression is larger than
c\gamma^{2}{\delta^{4}}
for some
c>0
.
Because of the factor
n^{-3/2}
in front and the energy bound, this implies that when the sum is restricted
to
|j^{\prime}|\geq\delta n
, the respective expression is of order
o_{n}(1)
. So we can write (
8.13
) as

(8.14)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)&=-\frac{\gamma}{2^{2}(n+1)^{3/2}}\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\pi\ell\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\sum_{|j^{\prime}|\leq\delta n}\frac{\sin^{2}\left(\pi k_{j^{\prime}}\right)}{\left(\frac{\pi\ell}{n}\right)^{2}\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)+2^{4}\gamma^{2}\sin^{4}\left(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\right)}\\
&\times\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1).\end{split}

Using the approximations
\sin\Big(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\Big)\approx\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}
and
\cos\Big(\frac{\pi k_{j^{\prime}}}{2}\Big)\approx 1
, valid for a sufficiently small
\delta
, we can rewrite it as

(8.15)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)&=-\frac{\gamma}{2^{2}n^{3/2}}\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\pi\ell\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\sum_{j^{\prime}=-\delta n}^{\delta n}\frac{\left(\pi k_{j^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}{\left(\frac{\pi\ell}{n}\right)^{2}+\gamma^{2}(\pi k_{j^{\prime}})^{4}}\\
&\times\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1)\\
=-\frac{\gamma}{2^{2}n^{1/2}}&\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}\pi\ell\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\frac{(\pi u)^{2}\mathrm{d}u}{\left(\frac{\pi\ell}{n}\right)^{2}+\gamma^{2}(\pi u)^{4}}\\
&\times\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1).\end{split}

Changing variables
v=\left(\frac{\pi\gamma n}{\ell}\right)^{1/2}u
we
conclude that

(8.16)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\theta_{pr,-}(\varphi^{\prime};n)&=-\frac{1}{2^{2}\pi\gamma^{1/2}}\sum_{|\ell|\leq n^{\kappa}}(\pi|\ell|)^{1/2}\widehat{(\varphi^{\prime})_{n,o}}(\ell)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{v^{2}\mathrm{d}v}{1+v^{4}}\\
&\times\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\big\langle\!\big\langle\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}\right)^{2}\big\rangle\!\big\rangle_{t}\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi u_{y}\ell)+o_{n}(1).\end{split}

Using the residue theorem one can calculate

(8.17)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{v^{2}\mathrm{d}v}{1+v^{4}}=\frac{1}{2^{3/2}}.

In Section
12.2
we prove the following

Proposition 8.2
.

For any
\varphi\in C_{c}(0,1)
,

(8.18)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{y=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{y}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left(\left(\nabla^{*}p_{y}(s)\right)^{2}-2\mathcal{E}_{x}(s)\right)\;\mathrm{d}s=0.

Then from (
8.17
) and (
8.18
) we conclude that

(8.19)
